Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer bases b>9 such that 17_b divides 97_b in base b notation. Interpret: 17_b is a number in base b: digits '1','7' in base b: value = 1*b + 7 = b + 7. Similarly 97_b = 9*b + 7. Wait digits 9 and 7: need base b>9 so digit 9 allowed. So 97_b = 9*b + 7.

We want (b + 7) divides (9*b + 7). That is (b+7) | (9b + 7). Compute 9b+7 mod (b+7). Let d = b+7. Then b = d - 7. Plug into 9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d -63 +7 = 9d -56. So we need d | 9d -56. Equivalent 9d -56 ≡0 mod d => -56 ≡0 mod d => d must divide 56. Also d> ? since base b>9 => b>9 => d = b+7 >16. So d divides 56 and d>16. Also base must be integer. We need integer d that divides 56, >16. Divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. >16: 28,56. So d ∈ {28,56}. Then b = d - 7: gives b = 21, 49. Both >9. Need integer bases. Are there any further constraints? Perhaps digits up to 9 only? In base 49 digits up to 48. So digit 9 allowed. So ok.

Also check that 17_b divides 97_b. Let's test.

For b=21: 17_b = 21+7=28. 97_b = 9*21+7=189+7=196? Wait 9*21=189, +7=196. 196 divisible by 28? 28*7=196. Yes.

For b=49: 17_b=56. 97_b=9*49+7=441+7=448. 448 divisible by 56? 56*8=448. Yes.

Thus both work. Sum of all integer bases >9: 21+49 = 70.

Answer: \boxed{70}.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
17_b = 1\cdot b + 7 = b+7, \qquad
97_b = 9\cdot b + 7.
\]

We need \(17_b\) to divide \(97_b\), i.e.

\[
(b+7)\mid(9b+7).
\]

Set \(d=b+7\) \(\;(b=d-7)\). Then

\[
9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d-56.
\]

Thus we require  

\[
d \mid (9d-56) \;\Longrightarrow\; 9d-56 \equiv 0 \pmod d
\;\Longrightarrow\; -56 \equiv 0 \pmod d
\;\Longrightarrow\; d\mid 56.
\]

The divisors of 56 are  

\[
1,\;2,\;4,\;7,\;8,\;14,\;28,\;56.
\]

Since the base satisfies \(b>9\), we have \(d=b+7>16\).  
Hence the admissible values of \(d\) are \(28\) and \(56\).

Corresponding bases:

\[
b = d-7 = 28-7 = 21,\qquad
b = 56-7 = 49.
\]

Both bases are valid (they exceed \(9\) and allow the digits 1,7,9).

Check:

- \(b=21:\; 17_{21}=28,\; 97_{21}=196\), \(196/28=7\).
- \(b=49:\; 17_{49}=56,\; 97_{49}=448\), \(448/56=8\).

Both satisfy the divisibility condition.

Hence the sum of all integers \(b>9\) that work is  

\[
21+49 = 70.
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Results

Correct Answer: 70

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
AB=AD+DE+EB=4+16+8=28 ,\qquad  
AC=AF+FG+GC=13+52+26 =91 .
\]

Put \(A=(0,0)\) and \(B=(28,0)\) on a horizontal line.
Let \(\theta\) be the angle between \(AB\) and \(AC\) and let  

\[
c=\cos \theta ,\quad s=\sin \theta \;(s>0).
\]

Then  

\[
C=(91c,\;91s),\qquad  
F=(13c,\;13s),\qquad  
G=(65c,\;65s).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Determining \(s=\sin\theta\)

Points \(D\) and \(E\) lie on \(AB\):

\[
D=(4,0),\qquad E=(20,0).
\]

The quadrilateral \(DEGF\) has vertices \(D(4,0)\), \(E(20,0)\),
\(G(65c,65s)\), \(F(13c,13s)\) in that order.
Its area by the shoelace formula is

\[
\frac12\Bigl|
 20(65s)+65c(13s)-13c(65s)\Bigr|
 =\frac12\Bigl|
 1248\,s\Bigr|
 =624\,s .
\]

The problem states \( \operatorname{Area}(DEGF)=288\); hence  

\[
624s = 288 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad s=\frac{288}{624}=\frac6{13}.
\]

Thus  

\[
\sin\theta=\frac6{13},\qquad 
\cos\theta=\sqrt{1-\frac{36}{169}}
          =\frac{\sqrt{133}}{13}\; (>0).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The reflected points

\[
M = 2F-D
   =(26c-4,\;26s),\\[2mm]
N = 2E-G
   =(40-65c,\;-65s).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

Vertices in counter‑clockwise order:

\[
A(0,0),\;
F(13c,13s),\;
N(40-65c,-65s),\;
B(28,0),\;
C(91c,91s),\;
E(20,0),\;
M(26c-4,26s).
\]

Using the shoelace formula,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sum x_i y_{i+1}&=
0+(-845cs)+0+2548s+0+520s+0
           =-845cs+3068s,\\[1mm]
\sum y_i x_{i+1}&=
0+(520s-845cs)+(-1820s)+0+1820s+0+0
           =520s-845cs .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{Area} 
 &= \frac12\Bigl|\sum x_i y_{i+1}-\sum y_i x_{i+1}\Bigr|\\[1mm]
 &= \frac12\Bigl|(-845cs+3068s)-(520s-845cs)\Bigr|\\
 &= \frac12\, (2548s)
 = 1274\,s .
\end{aligned}
\]

With \(s=\frac6{13}\),

\[
\mathrm{Area}=1274\cdot\frac6{13}
             =\frac{1274}{13}\cdot6
             =98\cdot 6
             =\boxed{588}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
*Remark*:  
The area of the heptagon turns out to equal the area of \(\triangle ABC\)
(\( \tfrac12 (28)(42)=588\)), so the two figures have exactly the same
contained area. This occurs because the reflections distribute the
extra region created by \(DEGF\) precisely along the sides of the
triangle.

For the 9 players let  

- \(c\) = number of chocolate cones,  
- \(v\) = number of vanilla cones,  
- \(s\) = number of strawberry cones.  

They must satisfy  

\[
c+v+s=9,\qquad c>v>s,\qquad c,v,s\ge 1 .
\]

---

### 1.  Enumerate the possible triples \((c,v,s)\)

1. **\(s=1\)**
   \[
   c+v+1=9\;\Rightarrow\; c+v=8,\;\;v\ge 2,\;c>v .
   \]
   \[
   v=2\Rightarrow c=6\;(6>2),\quad
   v=3\Rightarrow c=5\;(5>3).
   \]

2. **\(s=2\)**
   \[
   c+v+2=9\;\Rightarrow\; c+v=7,\;\;v\ge 3,\;c>v .
   \]
   \[
   v=3\Rightarrow c=4\;(4>3).
   \]

3. **\(s\ge 3\)**
   \[
   v\ge 4,\;c\ge 5,\;\;c+v=6
   \]
   has no solution with \(c>v\).

Thus we have only three valid triples:

\[
(6,2,1),\quad (5,3,1),\quad (4,3,2).
\]

---

### 2.  Count assignments for each triple

For distinct players, choosing which \(c\) players get chocolate, which \(v\) get vanilla, and the rest get strawberry is given by the multinomial coefficient  

\[
\frac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}.
\]

Compute:

| \((c,v,s)\) | \(\dfrac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}\) |

\]

\]

\boxed{16}

There are $8!=40320$ eight-digit positive integers that use each of the digits $1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8$ exactly once. Let $N$ be the number of these integers that are divisible by 22. Find the difference between $N$ and 2025.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need count how many permutations of digits 1-8 form a number divisible by 22. Observe 22 = 2 * 11. Divisible by 2 means last digit even. Digits 1-8 includes even digits 2,4,6,8. So last digit must be one of those. Additionally divisible by 11 means difference between sum of digits in alternating positions is multiple of 11. For an 8-digit number ABCDEFGH, property: (A+C+E+G) - (B+D+F+H) ≡ 0 mod 11. Since digits 1-8 sum 36. Let S1 = sum odd positions (1,3,5,7). Let S2 = sum even positions. Condition: S1 - S2 ≡ 0 mod 11 or equivalently S1 ≡ S2 mod 11. Since total sum 36, S1 + S2 = 36. So we need S1 ≡ 36 - S1 mod 11 → 2S1 ≡ 36 mod 11. 36 mod 11 = 3 (since 33). So 2S1 ≡ 3 (mod 11). 4! = 24, 3! = 6, product=144 * (#even digits in E).

So total N = 144 * sum over subsets S of (#even digits in complement E). But E = complement of S. (#even digits in E) = number of even digits (from {2,4,6,8}) not in S.

Thus for each subset, contribution = 144 * (4 - k). So N = 144 * Σ_{S} (4 - k), sum over all 4-element subsets with sum 18.

The number must be divisible by \(22=2\cdot 11\).

**Divisibility by \(2\)**  
The last digit must be even.  
The even digits available are \(\{2,4,6,8\}\).

**Divisibility by \(11\)**  
For an \(8\)-digit number \(a_1a_2\ldots a_8\),  
\[
(a_1+a_3+a_5+a_7)-(a_2+a_4+a_6+a_8)\equiv 0\pmod{11}.
\]

Let \(S_{\text{odd}}\) be the sum of the digits in the odd positions  
\((a_1,a_3,a_5,a_7)\).  
The total sum of the digits \(1+2+3+4+5+6+7+8=36\), so

\[
S_{\text{odd}}-(36-S_{\text{odd}})\equiv 0 \pmod{11} \implies
2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 36\equiv 3 \pmod{11}.
\]

The modular inverse of \(2\) mod \(11\) is \(6\); thus  
\(S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 3\cdot 6 \equiv 18\pmod{11}\).
The only possible value in the attainable range \(10\le S_{\text{odd}}\le 26\) is  
\[
S_{\text{odd}}=18.
\]

Therefore the four digits placed in odd positions must sum to \(18\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Counting suitable digit sets

We have to choose a 4‑element subset of \(\{1,2,\dots ,8\}\) whose sum is \(18\).

All such subsets are

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\{1,2,7,8\},\;\{1,3,6,8\},\;\{1,4,5,8\},\;\{1,4,6,7\},\\
&\{2,3,5,8\},\;\{2,3,6,7\},\;\{2,4,5,7\},\;\{3,4,5,6\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence there are \(8\) possible digit‑sets for the odd positions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Arranging the digits

For a given odd‑position set \(S\),

* The complementary set \(E\) (digits for even positions) has sum \(18\) as well.  
* Each set contains exactly two even digits (from \(\{2,4,6,8\}\)).  

Number of arrangements:

* Odd positions \(1,3,5,7\) can be filled by \(S\) in \(4! = 24\) ways.
* Even positions \(2,4,6,8\) must end in an even digit.  
  Choosing which even digit occupies position \(8\) gives \(2\) choices;  
  the remaining three digits can be arranged in \(3! = 6\) ways.

Hence for one subset \(S\)

\[
\text{arrangements}=4!\times 2\times 3! = 24\times 12 = 288.
\]

Since there are \(8\) such subsets, the total number \(N\) of
desired integers is  

\[
N = 8 \times 288 = 2304.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Required difference

\[
N-2025 = 2304 - 2025 = 279.
\]

\[
\boxed{279}
\]

assistant 
In an isosceles trapezoid that has an incircle, each side is tangent to the circle.  
For any tangential quadrilateral the sum of the lengths of opposite sides is equal:

\[
AB + CD = BC + DA .
\]

Let the parallel sides (bases) be \(r\) (shorter) and \(s\) (longer), and let the equal legs have length \(L\).
Taking the vertices \(A,B,C,D\) in order gives

\[
r+s = 2L \quad\Longrightarrow\quad L = \frac{r+s}{2}. \tag{1}
\]

The incircle has radius \(3\).  
The distance from its center to each base equals the radius, so the height \(h\) of the trapezoid is

\[
h = 2 \times 3 = 6. \tag{2}
\]

The area of the trapezoid is

\[
\frac{1}{2}(r+s)h = 72,
\]
\[
\frac12(r+s)(6)=72 \Longrightarrow r+s = 24. \tag{3}
\]

From (1) and (3):
\[
L = \frac{24}{2}=12. \tag{4}
\]

Let \(d\) be the horizontal shift from the top base to the bottom base on one side.  
For an isosceles trapezoid

\[
d = \frac{s-r}{2},
\]
and the leg \(L\) is the hypotenuse of a right triangle with height \(h\) and base \(d\):

\[
L^2 = h^2 + d^2.
\]

Using (4) and \(h=6\):

\[
12^2 = 6^2 + d^2 \Longrightarrow d^2 = 144-36 = 108
\]
\[
d = \sqrt{108} = 6\sqrt{3}.
\]

Therefore
\[
\dfrac{s-r}{2} = 6\sqrt{3} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad s-r = 12\sqrt{3}.
\]

Solving the system
\[
\begin{cases}
s+r = 24,\\[2pt]
s-r = 12\sqrt{3}
\end{cases}
\]
gives
\[
s = 12 + 6\sqrt{3},\qquad r = 12 - 6\sqrt{3}.
\]

Now compute \(r^2+s^2\):

\[
s^2 = (12+6\sqrt{3})^2 = 144 + 108 + 144\sqrt{3} = 252 + 144\sqrt{3},
\]
\[
r^2 = (12-6\sqrt{3})^2 = 144 + 108 - 144\sqrt{3} = 252 - 144\sqrt{3}.
\]

\[
r^2+s^2 = (252+144\sqrt{3}) + (252-144\sqrt{3}) = 504.
\]

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

assistant 
Let the letters be paired at random and in each pair the letters are written in alphabetical order to form a two‑letter word.  
The six words are then listed alphabetically.  
The last (lexicographically greatest) word is determined by the **first letter** of each word, because the words are ordered first by the first letter and, if necessary, by the second one.

---

### 1.  When does the last word contain \(G\)?

For the word containing \(G\) to be the last one, \(G\) has to be the first letter of that word.  
Hence \(G\) must be the **smaller** letter in its pair and this smaller letter must be
larger than the smaller letters of all the other five pairs.

All remaining letters are \(A,B,C,D,E,F,H,I,J,K,L\).

- The pair containing \(G\) must have a partner that is later than \(G\).  
  Thus the partner must be one of \(H, I, J, K, L\).

- Every other letter that is later than \(G\) (the other four of \(H,I,J,K,L\)) must
  be **larger** in its pair; otherwise the smaller letter of that pair would be
  greater than \(G\) and would make the last word, contradicting the requirement.

Consequently the remaining four high letters must each pair with a distinct low letter
from \(A,B,C,D,E,F\).  
The two remaining low letters will pair with each other.

---

### 2.  Counting favorable pairings

1. **Choose the partner of \(G\)**:  
   \(5\) choices (any of \(H,I,J,K,L\)).

2. **Choose the four low letters that pair with the remaining high letters**:  
   \(\binom{6}{4}=15\) ways.

3. **Match the four high letters to these four chosen low letters**:  
   \(4! = 24\) ways.

4. **Pair the two low letters left over**:  
   \(1\) way.

\[
N_{\text{fav}} = 5 \times 15 \times 24 \times 1 = 1800 .
\]

---

### 3.  Total number of pairings

The number of ways to partition \(12\) distinct letters into six unordered pairs is  

\[
N_{\text{tot}} = \frac{12!}{2!^6\,6!}= \frac{479001600}{46080}=10395 .
\]

---

### 4.  Probability

\[
P =\frac{1800}{10395}=\frac{40}{231}\qquad
(\text{after simplification}).
\]

---

The probability that the last word contains \(G\) is \(\dfrac{40}{231}\).  
Thus \(m=40,\;n=231\) and

\[
\boxed{271}
\]

The point of intersection satisfies  

\[
y=x^{2}-4,
\qquad 
(x,y)=R_{60^\circ}(u, v) \quad \text{with } v=u^{2}-4 .
\]

From the rotation formulas

\[
x=\frac{u-\sqrt3\,v}{2},\qquad 
y=\frac{\sqrt3\,u+v}{2},
\]

and eliminating \(u,\;v\) gives the equation for \(x\)

\[
3x^{4}+2\sqrt3\,x^{3}-25x^{2}-6\sqrt3\,x+40=0 .
\]

The unique solution in the fourth quadrant has \(x>0,\;y<0\).  
Solve this quartic by setting \(x=r+s\sqrt3\) with \(r,s\in\mathbb{Q}\).
Substituting into the equation yields the system  

\[
\begin{cases}
12rs(r^{2}+3s^{2})+2r^{3}+18rs^{2}-25\,2rs-6r=0,\\
3(r^{2}+3s^{2})^{2}+36r^{2}s^{2}+18r^{2}s+18s^{3}-25r^{2}-75s^{2}-18s+40=0 .
\end{cases}
\]

A rational solution is \(s=-\frac12\); inserting this gives  

\[
r^{2}=\frac{19}{4}\;\Longrightarrow\;r=\frac{\sqrt{19}}{2}.
\]

Thus  

\[
x=r+s\sqrt3=\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2}\;(>0).
\]

Hence  

\[
y=x^{2}-4=\left(\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2}\right)^{2}-4
   = \frac{11-\sqrt{57}}{2}-4
   = \frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}.
\]

So the required \(y\)-coordinate is  

\[
y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2},
\]

which has the form \(\dfrac{a-\sqrt b}{c}\) with
\(a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2\) (relatively prime).

Finally,

\[
a+b+c=3+57+2=62.
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

Let the blocks be the three \(3\times 3\) sub‑grids

\[
B_1=\begin{array}{ccc}
(1,1)&(1,2)&(1,3)\\(2,1)&(2,2)&(2,3)\\(3,1)&(3,2)&(3,3)
\end{array},\qquad 
B_2,\;B_3
\]

indexed by the block number (1,2,3), the row \(i\;(1\le i\le3)\) and the
column \(k\;(1\le k\le3)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Choice of the first block

A block must contain the digits 1–9 exactly once.
The number of ways to fill \(B_1\) is simply

\[
|B_1|=9!=362\,880 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Counting the second block

For a fixed filling of \(B_1\) let \(R_i\;(i=1,2,3)\) be the set of
digits that occur in row \(i\) of \(B_1\).
We require that in each row, the three digits of the second block are
**disjoint** from the three in the first block for the same row.

Let

\[
R_1=\{1,2,3\},\; R_2=\{4,5,6\},\; R_3=\{7,8,9\}
\]

(after a global renaming of the digits the situation is general).

Thus a valid second block \(B_2\) must satisfy

\[
B_i\subseteq ( \{1,\dots ,9\}\setminus R_i ),\qquad |B_i|=3,
\]
and \(B_1,B_2,B_3\) partition \(\{1,\dots ,9\}\).

Each digit is forbidden from the row in which it already appears in
\(B_1\); consequently every digit can be placed in exactly two of the
three rows of \(B_2\).

Let

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{digits 1–3} & \text{allowed rows for }B_2\ : 2,3\\
\text{digits 4–6} & \text{allowed rows for }B_2\ : 1,3\\
\text{digits 7–9} & \text{allowed rows for }B_2\ : 1,2
\end{array}
\]

Write \(a_2,a_3\) for the numbers counted from 1–3 that are put in
rows 2,3 respectively and similarly \(b_1,b_3\) from 4–6
and \(c_1,c_2\) from 7–9.
The conditions

\[
a_2+a_3=3,\qquad b_1+b_3=3,\qquad c_1+c_2=3,
\]
\[
b_1+c_1=3,\qquad a_2+c_2=3,\qquad a_3+b_3=3
\]

give \(4\) possible solutions

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
\text{case}&(a_2,a_3)&(b_1,b_3)&(c_1,c_2)\\ \hline
k=0 & (0,3)&(3,0)&(0,3)\\
k=1 & (1,2)&(2,1)&(1,2)\\
k=2 & (2,1)&(1,2)&(2,1)\\
k=3 & (3,0)&(0,3)&(3,0)
\end{array}
\]

For a fixed case \(k\), the actual numbers that go to the chosen rows can
be selected in

\[
\binom{3}{k}\binom{3}{3-k}\binom{3}{k}
\]

ways.  
Summing over \(k=0,1,2,3\) yields

\[
1+27+27+1=56
\]

different ways to decide **which digits go to which rows**.

Within each row these three digits can be arranged in \(3!\) ways,
independently for the three rows, giving another factor \(3!^3=216\).

Hence, for a fixed first block

\[
N_{\text{second}}=56\cdot 216=12\,096
\]

ways to fill the second block.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  The third block

Having two blocks, all digits that have not appeared in a given row
must appear in that row’s third block.
Consequently the third block is uniquely determined (apart from the
order of digits inside each row).

Again, each row of the third block can be arranged in \(3!\) ways,
so the third block contributes a factor \(3!^3=216\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total number of grids

\[
\#\text{grids}=9!\times N_{\text{second}}\times 3!^3
            =362\,880\times12\,096\times216
            =948\,109\,639\,680 .
\]

Factorising,

\[
948\,109\,639\,680
  =2^{16}\,3^{10}\,5\,7^{2}.
\]

Thus \(p,q,r,s\) are the distinct primes \(2,3,5,7\) and the
exponents \(a,b,c,d\) are \(16,10,1,2\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Requested expression

\[
p\,a+q\,b+r\,c+s\,d
=2\cdot16+3\cdot10+5\cdot1+7\cdot2
=32+30+5+14=81 .
\]

\[
\boxed{81}
\]

The graph of $f(x)$ has the sawtooth pattern. The parabola $x=34y^2$ intersects the graph of $f(x)$ at finitely many points. The sum of the $y$-coordinates of these intersection points can be expressed in the form $\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}$, where $a,b,c,$ and $d$ are positive integers, $a,b,$ and $d$ have greatest common divisor equal to 1, and $c$ is not divisible by the square of any prime. Find $a+b+c+d$.

assistant 
The function \(f\) is defined on \([-1,1)\) by \(f(x)=x\) and on \([1,3)\) by \(f(x)=2-x\).  
It is \(4\)-periodic: \(f(x+4)=f(x)\).  
Hence for any \(t\ge 0\) we can write  
\[
t=4k+r ,\qquad r\in[-1,3)
\]
and
\[
f(t)= \begin{cases}
r,& r\in[-1,1)\\[2pt]
2-r,& r\in[1,3)
\end{cases}
\]

The parabola is \(x=34y^2\).  
We need solutions of \(f(34y^2)=y\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Case 1: \(r\in[-1,1)\)

Then \(f(34y^2)=r=y\).  
Thus \(34y^2-4k-y=0\).  
Let \(n=k\). For integers \(n\ge0\), solutions of
\[
34y^2-y-4n=0
\]
are
\[
y=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+544n}}{68}.
\]

The roots are real for \(n\ge0\).  
Since the values must satisfy \(|y|<1\), \(n\) can only run up to \(n=8\).  
Hence \(n=0,1,\dots ,8\) give 18 solutions.  
For each \(n\), the two roots sum to \(\frac{1}{34}\); thus

\[
\sum_{\text{case 1}} y = 9\cdot\frac{1}{34}= \frac{9}{34}= \frac{18}{68}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Case 2: \(r\in [1,3)\)

Then \(r=2-y\) and \(f(34y^2)=2-r=y\).  
So
\[
34y^2-4k-2+y=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
34y^2+y- (4n+2)=0,\;n=k.
\]

For integers \(n\ge0\) the solutions are

\[
y=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{544n+273}}{68}.
\]

The larger root is positive, the smaller negative.
Both are admissible only when \(-1<y\le1\); this holds for  

- \(n=0,1,\dots ,8\) for the positive root,  
- \(n=0,1,\dots ,7\) for the negative root (for \(n=8\) the negative
  root is \(< -1\) and falls outside the range of \(f\)).

Hence we keep \(9\) positive and \(8\) negative roots: \(17\) values in total.

For each pair \(n\) the two roots sum to \(-\frac1{34}\); thus the total of all
roots for \(n=0,\dots ,8\) would be \(-\frac9{34}\).
We must discard the negative root coming from \(n=8\):

\[
y_{\,\text{neg},8}= \frac{-1-5\sqrt{185}}{68},
\]
because \(\sqrt{544\cdot8+273}= \sqrt{4625}=5\sqrt{185}\).

Therefore
\[
\sum_{\text{case 2}} y
   =-\frac{9}{34}-y_{\,\text{neg},8}
   =-\frac{9}{34}+\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}
   =\frac{-17+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Total sum of all \(y\)-coordinates

\[
S=
\sum_{\text{case 1}} y+\sum_{\text{case 2}} y
   =\frac{18}{68}+\frac{-17+5\sqrt{185}}{68}
   =\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\]

Thus the sum of the \(y\)-coordinates is  
\[
S=\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68},
\]
which is in the required form \(\frac{a+b\sqrt c}{d}\) with  

\[
a=1,\quad b=5,\quad c=185,\quad d=68.
\]

All conditions on \(a,b,c,d\) are satisfied
(\(\gcd(1,5,68)=1\) and \(185=5\cdot 37\) has no square prime factor).

Hence

\[
a+b+c+d = 1+5+185+68 = 259.
\]

\[
\boxed{259}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
The inequalities can be rewritten in terms of \(x\) and \(y\).

---

### 1.  Plane condition  
\(x+y+z=75\quad\Longrightarrow\quad z=75-x-y\).

---

### 2.  Reduce the inequalities  

Define  

\[
A=x-yz,\qquad B=y-zx,\qquad C=z-xy .
\]

*First inequality*  

\[
A<B \iff (x-y)(x+y-76)>0.
\]

This gives the two half‑planes

\[
\text{(I)}\; x>y,\;x+y>76 
\qquad\text{or}\qquad 
\text{(II)}\; x<y,\;x+y<76 .
\]

*Second inequality*  

\[
B<C \iff (x+1)(x+2y-75)<0.
\]

Equivalently,

\[
\begin{cases}
x>-1,\qquad y<\dfrac{75-x}{2},\\[4pt]
\text{or}\qquad
x<-1,\qquad y>\dfrac{75-x}{2}.
\end{cases}
\]

So \(B<C\) is the union of two opposite half‑planes bounded by the
vertical line \(x=-1\) and the sloping line \(x+2y=75\).

---

### 3.  Intersection of the two conditions  

Intersecting the half‑planes from (I) with those from \(B<C\) gives one
unbounded region; the intersection of (II) with the second branch of \(B<C\)
produces a bounded, convex region.

The bounded region is described by

\[
x>-1,\;x<y,\;x+y<76,\;y<\frac{75-x}{2}.
\]

Its boundary consists of the three lines  

* \(x=-1\)   (vertical line),  
* \(y=x\)    (the line \(x=y\)),  
* \(x+2y=75\) (the sloping line \(y=\frac{75-x}{2}\)).

Their intersection points are  

\[
(-1,-1),\qquad (-1,38),\qquad (25,25).
\]

These points form a triangle, the only finite convex region.

---

### 4.  Area of the triangle in the \((x,y)\)-plane  

Using the shoelace formula,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}_{XY} &=
\frac12\Bigl|
(-1)(38)+(-1)(25)+25(-1) \\
&\qquad-[-1(-1)+38(25)+25(-1)]
\Bigr|
=507.
\end{aligned}
\]

So the area on the plane \(x+y+z=75\) is larger by the factor
\(\sqrt{1+(-1)^2+(-1)^2}=\sqrt{3}\):
\[
\text{Area}_{\text{plane}} = 507\sqrt{3}.
\]

---

### 5.  Final answer  

The finite region’s area is \(507\sqrt{3}=a\sqrt{b}\) with  
\(a=507,\; b=3\).
Hence

\[
a+b = 507+3 = \boxed{510}.
\]

Answer: \boxed{\tfrac{487}{3}}.

Let's provide reasoning step by step.

Sampled message

assistant 
For every line segment (diameter or chord) that is drawn in the disk the number of
regions produced increases by  

\[
1+\text{(number of its interior intersections with previously drawn segments)} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Setting up the expectation

Let  

* \(S=27\)  – the total number of segments  
  (\(2\) diameters + \(25\) random chords),
* \(R\) – the final number of regions.

Starting with one whole disk,

\[
\mathbb E[R] = 1+\sum_{i=1}^{S}\bigl(1+\mathbb E[I_i]\bigr)
              =1+S+\sum_{i=1}^{S}\mathbb E[I_i].
\]

The term \(\sum\mathbb E[I_i]\) is the expected total number of
intersections among the \(S\) segments.
Hence

\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[R]=28+\text{(expected number of interior intersections)} } .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Intersections of the two diameters

The two perpendicular diameters intersect once in the centre of the disk.
Thus we add \(1\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Intersections of a chord with the diameters

For a chord its two endpoints are chosen uniformly on the circumference  
with the restriction that they lie in different quadrants.

There are four quadrants.  
A chord intersects a given diameter iff its endpoints lie in different
quadrants with respect to that diameter’s axis.

* with the \(x\)-axis (horizontal) the quadrants are  
  \((Q_1,Q_3), (Q_1,Q_4), (Q_2,Q_3), (Q_2,Q_4)\);  
  4 of the 6 possible unordered quadrant–pairs → probability \(4/6=2/3\).

* with the \(y\)-axis (vertical) the situation is the same; probability again  
  \(2/3\).

Thus a random chord meets each diameter with probability \(2/3\), giving

\[
\text{expected intersections per chord with the two diameters}
     = 2\cdot \frac23 =\frac43 .
\]

With \(25\) chords the expected number of intersections with the diameters is

\[
25\cdot\frac43 =\frac{100}{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Intersections between two random chords

Take two independent random chords \(AB\) and \(CD\) chosen under the same rule
(\(A,B\) in different quadrants; \(C,D\) also in different quadrants).
The probability that \(AB\) and \(CD\) intersect is \(1/3\).

*(Reason:* A set of four distinct points on a circle can be paired into two
chords in exactly three ways.
Because the ordering of the four points is uniform, each pairing is
equally likely; exactly one of the three pairings gives a crossing.
Hence \(P(\text{crossing})=1/3\).  
The quadrant restriction on each chord does not bias which pairing is chosen,
so the same probability \(1/3\) holds.)*

There are
\(\binom{25}{2}=300\) unordered pairs of chords, so the expected number of
intersections among the chords is

\[
300\cdot \frac13 = 100.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Expected total number of intersections

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{intersections}&=
  \underbrace{1}_{\text{diameter pair}}
  +\underbrace{\frac{100}{3}}_{\text{chords–diameters}}
  +\underbrace{100}_{\text{chord–chord}} \\[3pt]
 &= 101+\frac{100}{3}
 = \frac{303+100}{3}= \frac{403}{3}.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Expected number of regions

\[
\mathbb E[R] = 28 + \frac{403}{3}
              = \frac{84+403}{3}
              = \frac{487}{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle \frac{487}{3}}
\]

\[

\[

\]

Then  

\[
n^3=3^{3\alpha}u^3 .
\]

### 1.  \(v_3(a^3+b^3+c^3)\ge 7\)

Let \(m_{\min}=3\min(\alpha_a,\alpha_b,\alpha_c)\).

  Every term is already divisible by \(3^9\); the
  triple is automatically counted.

  \[
  N_1=27^3=19683 .
  \]

  \[
  3^{-6}\bigl(a^3+b^3+c^3\bigr)\equiv
   \sum_{\alpha_i=2}u_i^3 \pmod 3 .
  \]

  The sum must be \(0\bmod 3\).  
  \(\displaystyle
  \begin{cases}
    \alpha_i=2\Rightarrow u_i\equiv1 \text{ or }2\pmod3,\\
    \alpha_i\ge3\Rightarrow \text{contribution }\equiv0\pmod3 .
  \end{cases}\)

  Count the ordered triples:

    The two residues must be \(1\) and \(2\).  
    \[
    3\ \text{choices for the }\alpha\ge3\text{ place}
    \times 2\cdot27^2\ \text{ordered pairs of residues}
    \times 27\ \text{choices for the }\alpha\ge3\text{ value}
    =118098 .
    \]

  * **Three \(\alpha=2\)**  
    All residues must be the same \((1,1,1)\) or \((2,2,2)\).  
    \[
    2\cdot27^3=39366 .
    \]

  Hence  

  \[
  N_2=118098+39366=157\,464 .
  \]

  Let the non‑trivial contributions be

  \]
  A sum divisible by \(81=3^4\) requires the

  which is impossible because each is \(1\) or \(2\).  
  Thus there are **no** solutions in this case.

\[

assistant 
Let  

\[
d=n+2 \qquad (d>2)
\]

Then  

\[
n+3=d+1,\qquad n^2+9=(d-2)^2+9=d^2-4d+13 .
\]

The divisibility condition becomes  

\[
d \mid 3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13).
\]

Reduce the right–hand side modulo \(d\):

\[
d^2-4d+13 \equiv 13 \pmod{d}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad  
3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13) \equiv 3(d+1)\cdot13=39(d+1)\pmod{d}.
\]

Hence \(d\mid 39(d+1)\).  
Because \(\gcd(d,d+1)=1\), the factor \(d+1\) is coprime to \(d\).  
Therefore **\(d\) must divide 39**.

The positive divisors of \(39\) are \(1,\,3,\,13,\,39\).  
Since \(d>2\), we take \(d=3,\,13,\,39\).

Corresponding \(n=d-2\):

\[
n=1,\quad 11,\quad 37.
\]

All three satisfy the original condition (verified directly).

Sum of all such \(n\):

\[
1+11+37=49.
\]

\[
\boxed{49}
\]

Four unit squares form a $2 \times 2$ grid. Each of the 12 unit line segments forming the sides of the squares is colored either red or blue in such a way that each unit square has 2 red sides and 2 blue sides. Find the number of such colorings.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

Let each of the 12 edges of the \(2\times2\) grid be colored red or blue.  
Denote the internal vertical edges by \(a , b\), the internal horizontal edges by \(c , d\).  
The four remaining boundary edges in the top row are \(t_1,t_2\), in the bottom row \(b_1,b_2\), 
and in the left and right columns are \(l_1,l_2,r_1,r_2\).

For each small square the sum of its incident red edges must be exactly \(2\).  
Using red as \(1\) and blue as \(0\) we obtain

\[
\begin{aligned}
b_1+l_1 + (a+c)&=2 \qquad &(TL)\\
b_2+r_1 + (a+d)&=2 \qquad &(TR)\\
l_2+r_2+t_1 + (b+c)&=2 \qquad &(BL)\\
r_2+t_2 + (b+d)&=2 \qquad &(BR)
\end{aligned}
\]

Let  
\[
S_1=2-(a+c),\quad S_2=2-(a+d),\quad S_3=2-(b+c),\quad S_4=2-(b+d).
\]
Then the boundary variables satisfy

\[
\begin{cases}
b_1+l_1=S_1,\\
b_2+r_1=S_2,\\
l_2+r_2+t_1=S_3,\\[2pt]
r_2+t_2=S_4 .
\end{cases}
\]

The first two equations involve disjoint pairs; for a pair the number of solutions to
\(x+y=K\) is
\[
n(K)=
\begin{cases}
1 & K=0,\\
2 & K=1,\\
1 & K=2.
\end{cases}
\]
Hence the number of solutions for the first two pairs is \(n(S_1)\,n(S_2)\).

For the second group, \(r_2\) is shared. Taking \(r_2=0,1\) gives

\[
t_2=S_4-r_2\in\{0,1\},\qquad 
l_2+t_1=S_3-r_2\in\{0,1,2\},
\]
and the latter equation has \(n'(K)=n(K)\) solutions.  
Thus for fixed \(S_3,S_4\),

\[
N_B=\sum_{\substack{r_2=0,1\\ S_4-r_2\in\{0,1\}\\ S_3-r_2\in\{0,1,2\}}} n'(S_3-r_2).
\]

assistant 
For each factor in

\[
\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})},
\qquad 4\le k\le 63,
\]

write the logarithms in natural logs:

\[
\frac{\log_k 5^{k^2-1}}{\log_{k+1}5^{k^2-4}}
= \frac{\dfrac{(k^2-1)\ln 5}{\ln k}}
        {\dfrac{(k^2-4)\ln 5}{\ln (k+1)}}
= \frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\,\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

---

### 1.  Product of the algebraic fractions

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}= \frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}
               =\frac{k-1}{k-2}\cdot\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

Hence

\[
A=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\times
  \prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

The two products telescope.

**First product**

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}
= \frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{4}{3}\cdot\frac{5}{4}\cdots\frac{62}{61}
= \frac{62}{2}=31.
\]

**Second product**

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}
= \frac{5}{6}\cdot\frac{6}{7}\cdot\frac{7}{8}\cdots\frac{64}{65}
= \frac{5}{65}= \frac{1}{13}.
\]

Thus

\[
A=31\cdot\frac{1}{13}= \frac{31}{13}.
\]

---

### 2.  Product of the logarithmic ratios

\[
B=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
   =\frac{\ln5}{\ln4}\cdot\frac{\ln6}{\ln5}\cdots\frac{\ln64}{\ln63}
   =\frac{\ln64}{\ln4}.
\]

Since \(64=2^6\) and \(4=2^2\),

\[
B=\frac{6\ln2}{2\ln2}=3.
\]

---

### 3.  Entire product

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
= A\cdot B
=\frac{31}{13}\cdot3
= \frac{93}{13}.
\]

The fraction \(93/13\) is already in lowest terms (\(\gcd(93,13)=1\)).

So \(m=93\) and \(n=13\), giving

\[
m+n=93+13=106.
\]

\[
\boxed{106}
\]

assistant 
\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{Let } O\text{ be the centre of }\omega _2,\;  A\text{ the centre of }\omega _1,\\
R_1=6,\; R_2=15,\; AB=R_2-R_1=9.
\end{array}
\]

---

### 1.  Choosing a convenient coordinate system  

Place \(O=(0,0)\) and let the diameter \(BC\) lie on the \(x\)-axis.  
Then  

\[
B=(15,0),\qquad C=(-15,0).
\]

Since \(A\) is \(9\) units from \(O\) on the line \(OB\),

\[
A=(9,0),
\]

and an internal tangency gives \(A,B\) as described.

Because \(BC\) (horizontal) is perpendicular to \(AD\) we have

\[
AD \text{ is vertical through } A,
\]
hence \(D=(9,y)\).  
Because \(D\) lies on \(\omega_2\),

\[
9^2+y^{2}=15^2 \;\Longrightarrow\; y^2=144 \;\Longrightarrow\; y=\pm12.
\]

From the relation “\(C\) is closer to \(GH\) than to \(EF\)” and “\(D\) is closer to
\(FG\) than to \(EH\)” we know \(D\) must be above the rectangle, so

\[
D=(9,12).
\]

---

### 2.  Rectangle \(EFGH\) in \(\omega_1\)

Let the rectangle be centered at \(A\) (the circumcenter of the rectangle must be
the centre of the circle).  
Let

\[
p=\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{width}),\qquad q=\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{height}),
\]

so the vertices are

\[
E(9+p,-q),\; F(9+p,q),\;
G(9-p,q),\; H(9-p,-q).
\]

Because each vertex lies on \(\omega_1\) of radius \(6\),

\[
p^2+q^2=6^2=36. \tag{1}
\]

Since \(EF\) is the side perpendicular to \(BC\), \(EF\) is vertical,
hence \(EF\) is the right side of the rectangle.  
The left side \(GH\) is therefore the side closest to \(C\) – this matches the
given condition.

---

### 3.  Equality of the two triangles

Triangle \(DGF\): vertices \(D(9,12)\), \(G(9-p,q)\), \(F(9+p,q)\)  
- base \(GF=2p\) (horizontal),
- height \(12-q\).

\[
[ DGF]=\frac12\,(2p)(12-q)=p(12-q). \tag{2}
\]

Triangle \(CHG\): vertices \(C(-15,0)\), \(H(9-p,-q)\), \(G(9-p,q)\)  
- base \(HG=2q\) (vertical),
- horizontal distance from \(C\) to the base line \(x=9-p\) equals \(24-p\).

\[
[ CHG]=\frac12\,(2q)(24-p)=q(24-p). \tag{3}
\]

Given \([DGF]=[CHG]\),

\[
p(12-q)=q(24-p)\;\Longrightarrow\;12p=24q
\;\Longrightarrow\;\boxed{p=2q}. \tag{4}
\]

Substituting \(p=2q\) into (1):

\[
(2q)^2+q^2=36\quad\Longrightarrow\quad5q^{\,2}=36
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad q=\frac{6}{\sqrt5},\;
p=\frac{12}{\sqrt5}.
\]

---

### 4.  Area of the rectangle

\[
\text{Area}= (2p)(2q)=4pq
=4\left(\frac{12}{\sqrt5}\right)\left(\frac{6}{\sqrt5}\right)
=4\cdot\frac{72}{5}
=\boxed{\frac{288}{5}}.
\]

The fraction \(\frac{288}{5}\) is already in lowest terms.  
Let \(m=288,\; n=5\); thus \(m+n=288+5=\boxed{293}\).

Let $ A $ be the set of positive integer divisors of 2025. Let $ B $ be a randomly selected subset of $ A $. The probability that $ B $ is a nonempty set with the property that the least common multiple of its elements is 2025 is $ \frac{m}{n} $, where $ m $ and $ n $ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $ m + n $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need to find probability that a randomly selected subset of A (positive integer divisors of 2025) is non-empty and its LCM is 2025. We're selecting uniformly random subset of A. There are 2^{|A|} equally likely subsets (including empty). We need count of subsets whose lcm of elements equals 2025, then divide by 2^{|A|}. Also condition nonempty, but those subsets include empty maybe not counted.

First find prime factorization of 2025. 2025 = 2025/5=405, /5=81, gives 5^2*81=5^2*3^4. So 2025 = 3^4 *5^2.

The set of all divisors: exponents e3 between 0 and 4, e5 between 0 and 2. So number of divisors |A| = (4+1)(2+1)=5*3=15. Good.

We need number of subsets S of A (nonempty) such that lcm of elements of S is exactly 3^4*5^2.

Observations: For lcm to be N, each prime exponent must be achieved by at least one element in the subset. In other words, for each prime p, there must be at least one element whose exponent of p equals the maximal exponent in N (i.e., e3=4 for p=3 and e5=2 for p=5). But also, care about lower exponents? With lcm having exponent 4, we need no one element with exponent 4? Actually lcm over S of exponents = maximum over S. So to achieve exponent 4 we need at least one element with exponent 4. Also if subset contains no element with exponent 4 for 3, then lcm exponent <4. So need at least one divisors with 3^4 factor.

Analogous for 5^2.

Thus we need subsets that contain at least one divisor with exponent of 3 equal to 4 and at least one divisor with exponent of 5 equal to 2. Are there constraints related to both primes simultaneously? If we pick an element that has both 3^4 and 5^2 present, then that element alone would give lcm with both. But we could pick one element for 3 part and another for 5 part; but in all combos, each prime's max exponent must be achieved by some element.

Essentially we need subsets that, when considering projections onto the grid of exponents, satisfy that the maximum exponent for 3 among the chosen elements is 4, and maximum for 5 is 2.

So we need to count subsets of A that contain at least one "3-4" divisor (i.e., exponent 3=4), and at least one "5-2" divisor (exponent 5=2). But careful: there might be a subset that doesn't contain a "3-4" divisor but still lcm 3^4: Is it possible that lcm exponent 4 is achieved by combination even if none individually has exponent 4? Let's consider: LCM exponent is maximum of exponents across all elements. If all have exponent <=3, max <=3. So can't get 4. So at least one element must have exponent 4. Similarly for 5^2.

Thus we can model independent conditions: choose subsets with at least one element from set C3 = divisors with exponent of 3=4. And at least one element from set C5 = divisors with exponent of 5=2. Those sets may overlap when a divisor has both exponents at maximum: then it's in both sets. Let's compute sizes.

First total divisors: 15. Count of divisors with 3 exponent 4: fix e3=4, e5 can be 0,1,2: 3 possibilities. So |C3|=3.

Divisors with 5 exponent 2: fix e5=2, e3 can be 0...4: 5 possibilities. So |C5|=5.

Overlap: divisors with e3=4 and e5=2: only one element: 3^4 *5^2 =2025. So |C3∩C5|=1.

Set sizes: |C3|=3, |C5|=5, |intersection|=1.

Now we need count of subsets S such that S∩C3≠∅ and S∩C5≠∅. We also require nonempty subset. But if both intersect conditions hold, S automatically nonempty. But we can note that the only problematic case would be subset empty fails.

But to be safe: All counted subsets satisfy both intersection nonempty, so automatically nonempty.

We need to count number of subsets of A that contain at least one element from C3 and at least one element from C5. Equivalent to total number of subsets minus subsets missing at least one of these sets.

Let total subsets = 2^{15}.

Let A1 = subsets where S∩C3=∅ (missing all 3-4 elements). Count: choose any subset from A\C3 (size 15-3=12). So |A1|=2^{12}.

Let A2 = subsets where S∩C5=∅. Count: choose from A\C5 (size 15-5=10). |A2|=2^{10}.

Let A12 = subsets missing both? That is S∩C3=∅ and S∩C5=∅ => choose from A \ (C3∪C5). Compute size of C3∪C5: |C3|+|C5|-|intersection|=3+5-1=7. So there are 15-7=8 elements not in C3∪C5.

Thus |A12|=2^8.

We can use inclusion-exclusion: number of subsets violating at least one condition = |A1∪A2| = |A1|+|A2|-|A12| = 2^{12} + 2^{10} - 2^8.

Then number of subsets satisfying both conditions = total subsets - that = 2^{15} - (2^{12} + 2^{10} - 2^8) = 2^{15} - 2^{12} - 2^{10} + 2^8.

Also need to subtract empty set? The empty set is counted in "violating at least one condition" because it misses both, counted in A12. So it's already excluded. So we don't need further adjust.

Thus number of desired subsets = 2^8 (8?) Wait compute numeric maybe.

Let's compute powers:

2^8=256
2^10=1024
2^12=4096

assistant 
The positive divisors of \(2025\) have the prime factorization  

\[
2025=3^{4}\cdot 5^{2}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad |A|=(4+1)(2+1)=15 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  When does a subset \(S\subseteq A\) have LCM \(2025\)?

For a prime \(p\), the exponent in \(\operatorname{lcm}(S)\) equals the maximum exponent of \(p\) among the elements of \(S\).
Hence \(\operatorname{lcm}(S)=3^{4}\cdot5^{2}\) iff

* at least one element of \(S\) has exponent \(3\) equal to \(4\), and  
* at least one element of \(S\) has exponent \(5\) equal to \(2\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Divisors with the maximal exponents

\[
\begin{aligned}
C_3 &=\{ d\mid d\text{ has }3\text{-exponent }4\}      &&|C_3|=3  &(e_5=0,1,2)\\
C_5 &=\{ d\mid d\text{ has }5\text{-exponent }2\}      &&|C_5|=5  &(e_3=0,\dots,4)\\
C_3\cap C_5 &=\{ 3^{4}5^{2}=2025\}                    &&|C_3\cap C_5|=1
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Count of “good” subsets

Let \(S\) be a subset of \(A\).  
It satisfies the desired property iff \(S\cap C_3\neq\varnothing\) and \(S\cap C_5\neq\varnothing\).

Using inclusion–exclusion on the whole set \(A\ (|A|=15)\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\#\text{(subsets missing }C_3)&=2^{15-3}=2^{12},\\
\#\text{(subsets missing }C_5)&=2^{15-5}=2^{10},\\
\#\text{(subsets missing both)}&=2^{15-7}=2^{8}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= 2^{15}-\bigl(2^{12}+2^{10}-2^{8}\bigr)   \\
  &= 32768-4096-1024+256                      \\
  &= 27904.
\end{aligned}
\]

So \(N=27904\) subsets of \(A\) have LCM \(2025\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Probability

Every subset of \(A\) is equally likely; the total number of subsets is \(2^{15}=32768\).

\[
P=\frac{N}{2^{15}}=\frac{27904}{32768}
  =\frac{256\cdot109}{2^{15}}
  =\frac{109}{128}.
\]

Since \(\gcd(109,128)=1\), the fraction is already reduced.

The required sum \(m+n\) is

\[
m+n=109+128=237.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. He uses the so-called **greedy algorithm**, successively choosing the coin of greatest value that does not cause the value of his collection to exceed $ N $. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. However, this collection of 9 coins uses more coins than necessary to get a total of 42 cents; indeed, choosing 4 10-cent coins and 2 1-cent coins achieves the same total value with only 6 coins.

In general, the greedy algorithm succeeds for a given $ N $ if no other collection of 1-cent, 10-cent, and 25-cent coins gives a total value of $ N $ cents using strictly fewer coins than the collection given by the greedy algorithm. Find the number of values of $ N $ between 1 and 1000 inclusive for which the greedy algorithm succeeds.

assistant 
The greedy algorithm always takes as many 25‑cent coins as possible, then as many 10‑cent coins as possible, and finally uses 1‑cent coins for the rest.

Let  

\[
N = 25q+r ,\qquad 0\le r<25 \quad (\text{so }q\ge 0,\; r\text{ is the remainder mod }25).
\]

The greedy solution therefore contains:

* \(q\) 25‑cent coins,
* \(s=\lfloor r/10 \rfloor\) 10‑cent coins,
* \(t=r-10s\) 1‑cent coins.

Hence the number of coins used by greedy is  

\[
C_g = q+s+t = q + r - 9\lfloor r/10\rfloor \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  When is the greedy solution optimal?

If we reduce the number of 25‑cent coins by \(k\) (where \(k\ge 1\)), we must make up the value \(25k\) with 10‑cent and 1‑cent coins.  
Let  

\[
V=25k+r .
\]

The minimum number of 10‑cent and 1‑cent coins required for \(V\) is  

\[
f(V)=\left\lfloor \frac{V}{10}\right\rfloor + (V\bmod 10). \tag{2}
\]

Thus the total number of coins if we use \(q-k\) 25‑cent coins is  

\[
T_k=(q-k)+f(V). \tag{3}
\]

The greedy algorithm is **not** optimal iff for some \(k\ge 1\)

\[
T_k < C_g .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Checking the only possible case \(k=1\)

Because a 25‑cent coin gives a “savings” of \(24\) coins compared with an equivalent 1‑cent value, reducing one 25‑cent coin and trying to compensate with 10‑cent coins gives the biggest chance of improvement.  
If reducing one 25‑cent coin does not help, reducing two or more cannot help either.

Take \(k=1\):  

\[
V = 25+r, \qquad 
T_1 = (q-1)+f(25+r). \tag{4}
\]

Using (2) we evaluate \(f(25+r)\):

| \(r\) | \(V=25+r\) | \(\left\lfloor V/10\right\rfloor\) | \(V\bmod10\) | \(f(V)\) |
|------|-------------|-------------------|------------|--------|
| 0 | 25 | 2 | 5 | 7 |
| 1 | 26 | 2 | 6 | 8 |
| 2 | 27 | 2 | 7 | 9 |
| 3 | 28 | 2 | 8 |10 |
| 4 | 29 | 2 | 9 |11 |
| 5 | 30 | 3 | 0 | 3 |
| 6 | 31 | 3 | 1 | 4 |
| 7 | 32 | 3 | 2 | 5 |
| 8 | 33 | 3 | 3 | 6 |
| 9 | 34 | 3 | 4 | 7 |
|10 | 35 | 3 | 5 | 8 |
|11 | 36 | 3 | 6 | 9 |
|12 | 37 | 3 | 7 |10 |
|13 | 38 | 3 | 8 |11 |
|14 | 39 | 3 | 9 |12 |
|15 | 40 | 4 | 0 | 4 |
|16 | 41 | 4 | 1 | 5 |
|17 | 42 | 4 | 2 | 6 |
|18 | 43 | 4 | 3 | 7 |
|19 | 44 | 4 | 4 | 8 |
|20 | 45 | 4 | 5 | 9 |
|21 | 46 | 4 | 6 |10 |
|22 | 47 | 4 | 7 |11 |
|23 | 48 | 4 | 8 |12 |
|24 | 49 | 4 | 9 |13 |

Now compare \(T_1\) with \(C_g\) (1).  
Let \(s=\lfloor r/10\rfloor\) (so \(s=0,1,\text{or }2\)).
Substituting \(C_g = q+r-9s\) and \(T_1 = q-1+f(25+r)\):

\[
T_1-C_g = \bigl(f(25+r) - r +9s -1\bigr).
\]

From the table above this difference is  
\(6\) when \(r\in\{0,1,2,3,4,10,11,12,13,14,20,21,22,23,24\}\);  
and \(-3\) when \(r\in\{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}\).

Thus **reducing one 25‑cent coin improves the solution** precisely when  

\[
r \in \{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\},
\]

i.e. when \(N\bmod 25\) lies in those two blocks of five numbers.
For all other remainders, \(T_1-C_g\ge0\); a further reduction of 25‑cent coins can only worsen the coin count, so greedy is optimal.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Counting the values of \(N\) ( \(1\le N\le1000\) )

Let \(q=\lfloor N/25\rfloor\) and \(r=N\bmod 25\).

– If \(q=0\) (i.e. \(N<25\)), greedy is always optimal.

– For each \(q\ge 1\), the greedy algorithm fails exactly for  
  \(r=5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\); that is, \(10\) failures per full
  block of 25 numbers.

Within \(1\le N\le1000\) we have  

\[
\lfloor1000/25\rfloor = 40
\]

full 25‑number blocks, but the last block contains only the single number \(N=1000\) (where \(q=40, r=0\), so no failure).

Hence the total number of failures is

\[
39 \text{ blocks} \times 10 \text{ failures per block} = 390 .
\]

Therefore, the number of \(N\) for which the greedy algorithm succeeds is

\[
1000 - 390 = 610 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

assistant 
For

\[
f(x)=\sin \!\bigl(7\pi \sin 5x\bigr)
\]

the zeros satisfy  

\[
7\pi \sin 5x = k\pi \qquad (k\in\mathbb Z)
\]

hence  

\[
\sin 5x=\frac{k}{7}, \qquad  -1\le \frac{k}{7}\le 1
\]

so \(k=-7,-6,\dots ,6,7\), a total of \(15\) integers.

---

### Number of zeros \(n\)

The equation \(\sin 5x = c\) has

* two solutions per period of \(\sin 5x\) if \(|c|<1\),
* one solution per period if \(|c|=1\).

The period of \(\sin 5x\) is \(2\pi/5\), and \(0<x<2\pi\) contains \(5\) such periods.

| \(k\) | \(|k/7|\) | solutions per period | total solutions |
|-------|----------|----------------------|-----------------|
| \(\pm 7\) | \(1\) | \(1\) | \(5\) each |
| others (\(-6\le k\le6\), \(k\ne0\)) | \(<1\) | \(2\) | \(10\) each |
| \(k=0\) | \(0\)   | \(2\) | \(9\) (exclude \(x=0,\,2\pi\)) |

Adding them:

\[
n = 5+5 + 12\times10 + 9 = 140-1 = 139 .
\]

So \(\boxed{139}\) distinct zeros of \(f(x)\) lie in the open interval \((0,2\pi)\).

---

### Tangent points \(t\)

A zero is tangent to the \(x\)-axis iff \(f'(x)=0\) at that point.  
Compute

\[
f'(x)=35\pi\cos\!\bigl(7\pi \sin 5x\bigr)\,\cos 5x .
\]

At a zero of \(f\) we already have \(\sin(7\pi\sin5x)=0\), so  
\(\cos(7\pi\sin5x)=\pm 1 \ne 0\).  
Hence \(f'(x)=0\) requires

\[
\cos 5x=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+m\pi .
\]

Thus \(x=\frac{\pi}{10}+m\frac{\pi}{5}\) with \(m=0,1,2,3,4\); i.e., \(10\) points
(\(5\) where \(\sin5x=1\) and \(5\) where \(\sin5x=-1\)), all of which satisfy the
zero condition (\(\sin5x=\pm1=\frac{\pm 7}{7}\)).
No other \(k\) can satisfy \(\cos5x=0\) together with \(\sin5x=k/7\).

Hence \(t=10\).

---

\[
n+t = 139 + 10 = \boxed{149}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
The condition “no person sits next to two other people’’ means that a chosen chair cannot have two occupied neighbors.  
Hence a block of consecutive occupied chairs may contain at most two people.  
So among the 16 chairs we must choose 8 occupied positions such that there is never a run of three consecutive 1’s.

---

### 1.  Describe the pattern of occupied chairs  

- Partition the occupied chairs into blocks, each block consisting of 1 or 2 consecutive occupied chairs.  
- Let \(k\) be the number of blocks.  
- The sum of the sizes of the blocks must be \(8\).  
- Between any two consecutive blocks there has to be at least one empty chair.  

Thus for a fixed \(k\) we need

* choose the sizes of the \(k\) blocks (each 1 or 2) so that their total is 8,
* place the mandatory zeros between blocks,
* distribute the remaining zeros among the \(k+1\) gaps (before the first block, between blocks, after the last block).

---

### 2.  Number of block size choices for a given \(k\)

Let \(t\) be the number of blocks of size 2.  
If there are \(k\) blocks,
\[
2t+(k-t)=8 \;\Longrightarrow\; t=8-k .
\]
The number of ways to decide which of the \(k\) blocks are of size 2 is
\[
\binom{k}{\,t\,}= \binom{k}{\,8-k\,}.
\]

The admissible values of \(k\) satisfy:
\[
t=8-k\ge 0,\qquad t\le k \;\Longrightarrow\; 4\le k\le 8.
\]

---

### 3.  Number of ways to distribute the empty chairs

We have \(8\) empty chairs.  
Each of the \(k-1\) gaps between blocks must contain at least one empty chair, leaving
\[
8-(k-1)=9-k
\]
“extra’’ empty chairs to distribute freely among the \(k+1\) gaps (before the first block, after the last block, and the \(k-1\) gaps between blocks).

Let \(y_1,\dots ,y_{k-1}\) denote the extra chairs in those internal gaps.
Then
\[
z_0+z_k+y_1+\cdots+y_{\,k-1}=9-k,
\]
with all variables non‑negative.  
The number of solutions is
\[
\binom{(9-k)+(k+1)-1}{(k+1)-1}= \binom{9}{k}.
\]

---

### 4.  Total number of admissible seat arrangements

For each permissible \(k\),
\[
N_k=\binom{k}{\,8-k\,}\binom{9}{k}.
\]

Adding over \(k=4,5,6,7,8\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
N
&= \binom{4}{4}\binom{9}{4}
  + \binom{5}{3}\binom{9}{5}
  + \binom{6}{2}\binom{9}{6}
  + \binom{7}{1}\binom{9}{7}
  + \binom{8}{0}\binom{9}{8} \\[2mm]
&= 1\times126
 +10\times126
 +15\times84
 +7\times36
 +1\times9 \\[2mm]
&= 126 + 1260 + 1260 + 252 + 9 \\[2mm]
&= 2907 .
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 5.  Required remainder

\[
N \equiv 2907 \pmod{1000} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
N\equiv 907 \pmod{1000}.
\]

\[
\boxed{907}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
For \(k=1,2,\dots ,12\) let \(k\) be the step on the \(24\)-gon that determines the chord length.  
The graph consisting of all chords of step \(k\) has as vertices the \(24\) polygon vertices and as edges the pairs \((i,i+k)\pmod{24}\).

Let \(d=\gcd(k,24)\).  
Then this graph is a disjoint union of \(d\) cycles, each of length \(\displaystyle \frac{24}{d}\).

- If \(\frac{24}{d}\) is odd (this occurs only for \(k=8\)), no perfect matching exists.
- Otherwise each cycle is even, and a cycle of even length has exactly \(2\) perfect matchings.  
  Consequently the whole graph has \(2^d\) perfect matchings.

The case \(k=12\) is special: the graph consists of \(12\) disjoint edges (each vertex has a unique partner), so there is exactly \(1\) matching.

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
k & d=\gcd(k,24) & \text{number of matchings} \\\hline
1 & 1 & 2 \\
2 & 2 & 2^2=4 \\
3 & 3 & 2^3=8 \\
4 & 4 & 2^4=16 \\
5 & 1 & 2 \\
6 & 6 & 2^6=64 \\
7 & 1 & 2 \\
8 & 8 & 0 \\
9 & 3 & 8 \\
10& 2 & 4 \\
11& 1 & 2 \\
12& \text{special} & 1
\end{array}
\]

Adding all possibilities:

\[
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+0+8+4+2+1 = 113 .
\]

Hence the number of ways to pair the \(24\) vertices by equal‑length chords is  

\[
\boxed{113}.
\]

The conditions give  

\[
r_i r_{i+1}=\frac{26}{5}\qquad (2\le i\le 10)
\]

Let \(a=r_2,r_4,r_6,r_8,r_{10}\) and \(b=r_3,r_5,r_7,r_9,r_{11}\).  
Thus \(ab=\dfrac{26}{5}\).

For any consecutive pair \(A_i,A_{i+1}\) the length is  

\[
\ell=\sqrt{a^2+b^2-\frac{624}{65}}
      =\sqrt{(a+b)^2-2ab-\frac{624}{65}}
      =\sqrt{(a+b)^2-20},
\]
because \(2ab=\dfrac{52}{5}=\frac{624}{65}=10.4\).

The perimeter is  

\[
P=a+b+9\ell=20 .
\]

Let \(s=a+b\).  Then \(\ell=\sqrt{s^2-20}\) and  

\[
s+9\sqrt{s^2-20}=20 .
\]

Set \(x=\sqrt{s^2-20}>0\).  
Then \(s=20-9x\) and

\[
(20-9x)^2=x^2+20\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
4x^2-18x+19=0 .
\]

Hence  

\[
x=\frac{18\pm\sqrt{20}}{8}=\frac{9\pm\sqrt5}{4}\quad(\text{only the smaller root works}).
\]

Taking \(x=\dfrac{9-\sqrt5}{4}\), we obtain  

\[
s=20-9x=\frac{-1+9\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

Thus  
\[
A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}=a+b=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

This is of the requested form with  

\[
m=9,\ \ n=5,\ \ p=1,\ \ q=4.
\]

Finally,  

\[
m+n+p+q = 9+5+1+4 = 19.
\]

\[
\boxed{19}
\]

Let define u_k = (1+x_k)/(1-x_k). Then x_k = (u_k -1)/(u_k +1). This is common to convert hyperbolic functions.

Compute x_k+1/x_k? Might simplify.

Compute 1/x_k = (u_k +1)/(u_k -1). Sum: x_k + 1/x_k = (u_k -1)/(u_k +1) + (u_k+1)/(u_k -1) = combine: ( (u_k-1)^2 + (u_k+1)^2 ) / (u_k^2 -1 ). Evaluate numerator: (u^2 -2u +1)+(u^2 +2u +1)=2u^2+2. Denominator = u^2-1. So sum = (2u^2+2)/(u^2-1)= 2(u^2+1)/(u^2-1).

Now recurrence: x_{k+1} = (1/3)(x_k + 1/x_k -1) => x_{k+1} = (1/3)( 2(u_k^2+1)/(u_k^2-1) -1 )= (1/3)( (2(u^2+1) - (u^2-1))/ (u^2-1) ) = (1/3)( (2u^2+2 - u^2 +1)/(u^2-1) ) = (1/3)( (u^2 +3)/ (u^2-1) ).

So x_{k+1} = (u_k^2 +3) / (3(u_k^2 -1)).

But we also have expression for x_{k+1} in terms of u_{k+1}: x_{k+1} = (u_{k+1} -1)/(u_{k+1}+1). So equate:

(u_{k+1} -1)/(u_{k+1} +1) = (u_k^2 +3)/(3(u_k^2 -1)).

Solve for u_{k+1}. Cross-multiply:

(u_{k+1} -1)*3(u_k^2 -1) = (u_{k+1}+1)(u_k^2 +3).

Expand: 3(u_k^2 -1) u_{k+1} -3(u_k^2 -1) = (u_k^2 +3) u_{k+1} + (u_k^2 +3).

Bring terms:

Left - right: [3(u_k^2 -1) u_{k+1} - (u_k^2 +3) u_{k+1}] + [-3(u_k^2 -1) - (u_k^2 +3)] = 0.

Factor u_{k+1}: u_{k+1} [3(u_k^2 -1) - (u_k^2 +3)] + [ -3(u_k^2 -1) - (u_k^2 +3) ] =0.

Compute coefficient: 3(u_k^2 -1) - (u_k^2 +3) = 3u_k^2 -3 - u_k^2 -3 = 2u_k^2 -6 = 2(u_k^2 -3).

Constant term: -3(u_k^2 -1) - (u_k^2 +3) = -3u_k^2 +3 - u_k^2 -3 = -4u_k^2.

Thus equation: u_{k+1} * 2(u_k^2 -3) - 4 u_k^2 = 0 => u_{k+1} = [4 u_k^2]/[2(u_k^2 -3)] = [2 u_k^2]/(u_k^2 -3).

Great! So recurrence simplifies to u_{k+1} = 2 u_k^2/(u_k^2 -3).

Sampled message

assistant 
\[
x_{k+1}=\frac{1}{3}\Bigl(x_k+\frac1{x_k}-1\Bigr)
\]

Set  

\[
u_k=\frac{1+x_k}{1-x_k}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
x_k=\frac{u_k-1}{u_k+1}.
\]

With this substitution

\[
x_{k+1}=\frac{2u_k^2}{3(u_k^2-3)} ,
\]

hence

\[
u_{k+1}= \frac{2u_k^2}{\,u_k^2-3\,}.
\]

Let \(u_k=a_k/b_k\) with \(\gcd(a_k,b_k)=1\).  
Then

\[
a_{k+1}=2a_k^2,\qquad
b_{k+1}=a_k^2-3b_k^2 .
\]

For the original fraction we have  

\[
x_k=\frac{a_k-b_k}{a_k+b_k}\quad\text{so}\quad
m_k=n_k
\;(\text{after reduction})\;
\Leftrightarrow
m_k+n_k=\frac{2a_k}{\gcd(a_k-b_k,a_k+b_k)} .
\]

A simple calculation shows that  

\[
\gcd(a_k-b_k,a_k+b_k)=3^{\,2^{k-1}-1},\qquad (k\ge1)
\]

and that the power of \(2\) in \(a_k\) equals \(2^{\,k}-1\).  
Consequently

\[
a_k = 2^{\,2^{k}-1}\cdot 9^{\,2^{k-1}},\qquad
m_k+n_k
   = \frac{2a_k}{3^{\,2^{k-1}-1}}
   = 2^{\,2^{k}}\;3^{\,2^{k-1}+1}.
\]

We need the value of this number for \(k=2025\) modulo \(1000\).

---

### Modulo \(8\)

\(2^{\,2^{2025}}\) contains the factor \(2^{\,2^{2025}}\ge 8\), hence  

\[
m_{2025}+n_{2025}\equiv 0\pmod{8}.
\]

---

### Modulo \(125\)

Using Euler’s theorem (\(\phi(125)=100\))

\[
\begin{aligned}
2^{\,2^{2025}}\! &\equiv 2^{\,e_1}\pmod{125},
      \qquad e_1=2^{2025}\pmod{100}\equiv 2^{5}=32,\\
2^{32} &\equiv 46\pmod{125},
\\[2mm]
3^{\,2^{2024}+1}\! &\equiv 3^{\,e_2}\pmod{125},
      \qquad e_2=2^{2024}+1\equiv 2^{4}+1=17,\\
3^{17} &\equiv 38\pmod{125}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
m_{2025}+n_{2025}\equiv 46\cdot 38
   =1748\equiv 123\pmod{125}.
\]

---

### Chinese Remainder Theorem

Let

\[
x\equiv 0\pmod 8,\qquad x\equiv 123\pmod{125}.
\]

Write \(x=8k\).  
The inverse of \(8\bmod 125\) is \(47\) because \(8\cdot47\equiv1\pmod{125}\).  
Thus

\[
k\equiv 123\cdot47\equiv 26\pmod{125}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x=8k\equiv 8\cdot 26=248\pmod{1000}.
\]

---

\[
\boxed{248}
\]

Let  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(b,0),\qquad C=(0,c)
\]

with \( \angle A=90^\circ\) and \(BC=38\), so

\[
b^2+c^2=38^2=1444 .
\]

Points \(K\) and \(L\) satisfy  

\[
AK=AL=BK=CL=KL=14 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Coordinates of \(K\) and \(L\)

From \(AK=BK\) the point \(K\) is on the perpendicular bisector of \(\overline{AB}\), hence  

\[
K=\Bigl(\frac{b}{2},\,k\Bigr),\qquad k>0 .
\]
Using \(AK=14\),

\[
\left(\frac{b}{2}\right)^2+k^2=196 \Longrightarrow k^2=196-\frac{b^2}{4}. \tag{1}
\]

Analogously, \(L\) lies on the perpendicular bisector of \(\overline{AC}\):

\[
L=\bigl(\ell,\frac{c}{2}\bigr), \qquad \ell>0,
\]
with

\[
\ell^2=196-\frac{c^2}{4}. \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The condition \(KL=14\)

\[
(\tfrac{b}{2}-\ell)^2+\bigl(k-\tfrac{c}{2}\bigr)^2=196 .
\]

Insert (1) and (2). After simplifying the cross–terms cancel and we obtain

\[
b\,\ell+c\,k=196 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
b\sqrt{196-\frac{c^2}{4}}
+c\sqrt{196-\frac{b^2}{4}}=196 . \tag{3}
\]

Let \(u=b^{\,2}\), \(v=c^{\,2}\) (both positive).  
Then \(u+v=1444\) and (3) becomes

\[
\sqrt{u}\,\sqrt{196-\frac{v}{4}}
+\sqrt{v}\,\sqrt{196-\frac{u}{4}}=196 .
\]

Squaring twice and simplifying leads to

\[
u\,v=519\,168 .
\]

With \(u+v=1444\) we solve

\[
t^2-1444t+519\,168=0 ,
\]
whose discriminant is \(92^2\).  Hence

\[
\{u,v\}=\{768,\;676\}.
\]

Thus

\[
(b,c)=(\sqrt{768}=16\sqrt3,\; \sqrt{676}=26)
\quad\text{or}\quad
(b,c)=(26,\;16\sqrt3).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Coordinates of the points

Take \(b=16\sqrt3,\;c=26\):

\[
B=(16\sqrt3,0),\qquad
K=\bigl(8\sqrt3,\,2\bigr),\qquad
L=\bigl(3\sqrt3,\,13\bigr),\qquad
C=(0,26).
\]

(The other ordering gives the same quadrilateral up to reflection.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Area of \(BKLC\)

Using the shoelace formula for the polygon \(B\!-\!K\!-\!L\!-\!C\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
S &= (16\sqrt3)(2)+(8\sqrt3)(13)+(3\sqrt3)(26)-26(16\sqrt3)\\
  &= 32\sqrt3+104\sqrt3+78\sqrt3-416\sqrt3\\
  &= -208\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

The area is

\[
\text{Area}=\frac12|S|=\frac12(208\sqrt3)=104\sqrt3 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Thus the area of \(BKLC\) is \(n\sqrt3\) with  

\[
\boxed{n=104}
\]

The function  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x}\qquad (x>0)
\]

has critical points at the zeros of  

\[
P(x)=x\,h'(x)-h(x)=0,
\]

where \(h(x)=(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)\).  

Writing  
\(h(x)=x^4-S_1x^3+S_2x^2-S_3x+S_4\)
with  
\(S_1=188+k,\; S_2=10116+188k,\; S_3=127008+10116k,\; S_4=127008k\),

we get  

\[
P(x)=3x^{4}-(376+2k)x^{3}+(10116+188k)x^{2}-127008k=0.   \tag{1}
\]

For \(f\) to attain its global minimum at exactly two distinct positive
values, the equation \(f(x)=m\) must have two double roots; hence  

\[
h(x)-mx=(x-a)^{2}(x-b)^{2}
\]

for some \(a,b>0\).  Comparing coefficients gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
S_1 &=2(a+b), \\
S_2 &=a^{2}+4ab+b^{2},\\
S_4 &=a^{2}b^{2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Let  
\(u=a+b,\qquad v=ab\).  
Then  

\[
k=2u-188, \qquad
u^{2}+2v =376u-25228, \qquad
v^{2}=254016u-23\,877\,504 .
\]

Eliminating \(v\) yields a quartic in \(u\):

\[
u^{4}-752u^{3}+191\,832u^{2}-19\,987\,520u+731\,962\,000=0.
\]

Factoring,

\[
(u-350)(u-98)(u-110)(u-194)=0 .
\]

Hence  
\(u=98,110,194,350\).  
Corresponding \(k=2u-188\) are

\[
k_1=2(98)-188=8,\qquad 
k_2=2(110)-188=32,\qquad 
k_3=2(194)-188=200,\qquad 
k_4=2(350)-188=512 .
\]

For each of the first three cases, the quadratic \(t^{2}-ut+v=0\)
has two positive roots (\(a,b\)), giving two distinct positive minima of
\(f\).  The fourth value gives a negative product \(v\), so only one
positive critical point and hence only one minimum.

Therefore the desired values of \(k\) are \(8,\;32,\;200\).

Finally, the sum of these three values is

\[
\boxed{240}.
\]
